I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the minimum number with distinct digits whose sum equals a given value `s`.

Key observations:
1. All digits must be distinct (0-9, but 0 can't be the first digit)
2. We want the minimum number, which means we want the smallest possible number of digits, and among those, the lexicographically smallest arrangement
3. Since we want the minimum number, we should try to use smaller digits first, but we need to ensure the sum is exactly `s`

## Strategy

To get the minimum number:
1. We want to use as few digits as possible (to make the number smaller)
2. Among numbers with the same digit count, we want the lexicographically smallest one

However, there's a constraint: digits must be distinct.

Let me think about the optimal approach:
- We should use larger digits towards the right (to minimize the overall value)
- We should use smaller digits towards the left (to minimize the overall value)

Wait, that's backwards. To get the minimum number, we want smaller digits on the left.

Actually, to minimize the number:
- We want to use as few digits as possible (fewer digits = smaller number)
- Among numbers with the same length, we want the digits to be in ascending order (smallest first)

But we need to be careful about the sum constraint.

Let me reconsider: to minimize the number, we should:
1. Try to form the number with the fewest possible digits
2. For a fixed number of digits, arrange them in ascending order (to get the minimum value)

So the algorithm:
1. Try different numbers of digits from 1 to 10 (since we have digits 0-9)
2. For each possible digit count k, check if we can form a sum of s using k distinct digits
3. We want the minimum number, so we start with the smallest possible k and find the actual digits

But how to find the digits? For a fixed k, to get the minimum number, we want the digits to be as small as possible from left to right, which means we should use small digits first.

Actually, to minimize the number, we want the leftmost digits to be as small as possible, so we should:
- Use the smallest possible digits starting from left, ensuring the sum constraint

But we need to ensure we can achieve sum s with distinct digits.

Alternative approach:
- We want to use the smallest digits possible, but we need to make sure the sum is exactly s
- We can greedily assign digits from left to right (smallest first) but this might not work

Let me think about it differently:

To minimize the number:
1. We want to use the fewest digits possible (since fewer digits means a smaller number)
2. For a fixed number of digits, we want the lexicographically smallest arrangement

So:
1. Find the minimum number of digits needed to achieve sum s with distinct digits
2. Then find the lexicographically smallest arrangement of distinct digits that sum to s

How to find the minimum number of digits?
- The maximum sum with k distinct digits is 9+8+7+...+(10-k) = sum from 10-k to 9
- The minimum sum with k distinct digits is 0+1+2+...+(k-1) = k*(k-1)/2

But we need to be careful - for k digits, the minimum sum is 0+1+2+...+(k-1) = k*(k-1)/2
The maximum sum is 9+8+7+...+(10-k) = 9+8+...+(10-k) = sum of arithmetic series.

Actually, the maximum sum with k distinct digits is 9+8+7+...+(10-k) = (k/2) * (9 + (10-k)) = (k/2) * (19-k)

For a given sum s, we need to find the smallest k such that there exists a set of k distinct digits that sum to s.

But actually, since we want minimum number, we want minimum k first, then lexicographically smallest arrangement.

Wait, is that true? For example, with sum 20:
- Can we achieve sum 20 with 2 digits? Max sum with 2 distinct digits is 9+8=17, so no
- Can we achieve sum 20 with 3 digits? Max is 9+8+7=24, min is 0+1+2=3, so yes (e.g., 3+8+9=20)
- So k=3 is minimum possible

Then we need to find the lexicographically smallest 3-digit number with distinct digits summing to 20.

To minimize the number, we want the leftmost digit to be as small as possible, then the next, etc.

So algorithm:
1. Find the minimum number of digits k needed to achieve sum s
2. For that k, find the lexicographically smallest arrangement of k distinct digits that sum to s

How to find the minimum k?
- For k=1: sum must be between 1 and 9 (since digits 0-9, but 0 gives sum 0 which is not >=1)
- For k=2: sum between 1+2=3 and 8+9=17
- For k=3: sum between 0+1+2=3 and 7+8+9=24
- ...
- For k=10: sum between 0+1+...+9=45 and 0+1+...+9=45 (only one combination)

Actually, the maximum sum with k digits is sum of largest k digits: 9+8+...+(10-k)
The minimum sum with k digits is sum of smallest k digits: 0+1+...+(k-1)

So we need the smallest k such that:
min_sum(k) ≤ s ≤ max_sum(k)

We can iterate k from 1 to 10 until we find such k.

Once we have k, how to find the lexicographically smallest arrangement?

We want to assign digits from left to right (most significant to least), and for each position, we want to use the smallest possible digit that allows us to complete the sum with remaining digits.

Algorithm for fixed k:
1. Start with an empty list of digits
2. For each position i from 0 to k-1:
   - Try digits from 0 to 9, but skip digits already used
   - For each candidate digit d, check if there exists a valid set of remaining (k-1-i) digits that sum to (s - current_sum - d)
   - The remaining digits must be distinct, unused, and their sum must be exactly (s - current_sum - d)
   - We can check if this is possible by verifying that the minimum possible sum of remaining digits is ≤ required sum ≤ maximum possible sum

Minimum sum for remaining r digits: sum of smallest r unused digits
Maximum sum for remaining r digits: sum of largest r unused digits

So we can greedily choose the smallest possible digit at each position, as long as a valid completion is possible.

Let me implement this:

```python
def can_achieve(remaining_sum, remaining_digits):
    # Can we achieve 'remaining_sum' with 'remaining_digits' distinct digits?
    if remaining_digits == 0:
        return remaining_sum == 0
    
    # Minimum sum with remaining_digits digits (smallest available)
    # We'll assume digits are from 0 to 9, and we can choose any distinct digits
    # The smallest possible sum is 0+1+2+...+(remaining_digits-1) = remaining_digits*(remaining_digits-1)//2
    min_sum = remaining_digits * (remaining_digits - 1) // 2
    
    # The maximum sum is 9+8+...+(10-remaining_digits) = sum of largest remaining_digits digits
    # = (9 + (10 - remaining_digits)) * remaining_digits // 2
    max_sum = (9 + (10 - remaining_digits)) * remaining_digits // 2
    
    return min_sum <= remaining_sum <= max_sum

def solve(s):
    # Find minimum k such that sum s can be achieved with k distinct digits
    k = 1
    while k <= 10:
        min_possible_sum = k * (k - 1) // 2
        max_possible_sum = (9 + (10 - k)) * k // 2
        
        if min_possible_sum <= s <= max_possible_sum:
            break
        k += 1
    
    # Now find the lexicographically smallest arrangement of k distinct digits summing to s
    digits = []
    used = [False] * 10
    remaining_sum = s
    
    for i in range(k):
        found = False
        # Try digits from 0 to 9, in ascending order
        for d in range(10):
            if used[d]:
                continue
                
            # Check if we can complete with the remaining digits
            remaining_digits = k - 1 - i
            if remaining_digits == 0:
                # This is the last digit, just need to check if sum matches
                if d == remaining_sum:
                    digits.append(d)
                    used[d] = True
                    found = True
                    break
            else:
                # Check if we can achieve (remaining_sum - d) with (remaining_digits) distinct digits
                needed_sum = remaining_sum - d
                if can_achieve(needed_sum, remaining_digits):
                    digits.append(d)
                    used[d] = True
                    found = True
                    break
        
        if not found:
            # This shouldn't happen given our k selection
            break
    
    # Convert digits to number (but need to handle leading zeros)
    # Since we're building from left to right, and we have k digits, the first digit shouldn't be 0
    # But if our k=1 and s=0, this would be a problem, but s >= 1
    
    # The first digit should be at least 1
    # In our algorithm, if we have k>=2, and we start with 0, that's bad
    
    # Let's fix this: for the first digit, we should skip 0
    # We need to modify the algorithm
    
    # Actually, let's restart with a better approach
    
    # For k=1: digits must be s itself, so if 1<=s<=9, answer is s
    # For k>=2: first digit cannot be 0
    
    # Let's implement a corrected version
    
    # Actually, let me rewrite the algorithm to handle the first digit properly
    
    digits = []
    used = [False] * 10
    remaining_sum = s
    
    for i in range(k):
        found = False
        # For first digit (i=0), we cannot use 0
        start_from = 0 if i == 0 else 1
        for d in range(start_from, 10):
            if used[d]:
                continue
                
            # Check if we can complete with remaining digits
            remaining_digits = k - 1 - i
            if remaining_digits == 0:
                if d == remaining_sum:
                    digits.append(d)
                    used[d] = True
                    found = True
                    break
            else:
                needed_sum = remaining_sum - d
                if can_achieve(needed_sum, remaining_digits):
                    digits.append(d)
                    used[d] = True
                    found = True
                    break
        
        if not found:
            # This shouldn't happen
            break
    
    # Convert to number
    result = 0
    for d in digits:
        result = result * 10 + d
    
    return result
```

Wait, I see an issue in my `can_achieve` function. The maximum sum calculation is wrong.

For remaining_digits digits, the maximum sum is the sum of the largest remaining_digits digits from 0-9.

The largest digits are 9,8,7,..., so the maximum sum is sum from (10-remaining_digits) to 9.

This sum = (number of terms) * (first + last) / 2 = remaining_digits * ((10 - remaining_digits) + 9) / 2 = remaining_digits * (19 - remaining_digits) / 2

Let me fix that:

```python
def can_achieve(remaining_sum, remaining_digits):
    if remaining_digits == 0:
        return remaining_sum == 0
    
    # Minimum sum: sum of smallest remaining_digits digits: 0+1+2+...+(remaining_digits-1)
    min_sum = remaining_digits * (remaining_digits - 1) // 2
    
    # Maximum sum: sum of largest remaining_digits digits: (10-remaining_digits) + (11-remaining_digits) + ... + 9
    # This is sum from (10-remaining_digits) to 9
    # = (remaining_digits) * (first + last) / 2
    # first = 10 - remaining_digits, last = 9
    max_sum = remaining_digits * (10 - remaining_digits + 9) // 2
    
    return min_sum <= remaining_sum <= max_sum
```

Final implementation:

```python
def can_achieve(remaining_sum, remaining_digits):
    if remaining_digits == 0:
        return remaining_sum == 0
    
    # Minimum sum: sum of smallest remaining_digits digits (0 to remaining_digits-1)
    min_sum = remaining_digits * (remaining_digits - 1) // 2
    
    # Maximum sum: sum of largest remaining_digits digits (from (10-remaining_digits) to 9)
    max_sum = remaining_digits * (10 - remaining_digits + 9) // 2
    
    return min_sum <= remaining_sum <= max_sum

def solve(s):
    # Find minimum k such that sum s can be achieved with k distinct digits
    k = 1
    while k <= 10:
        min_possible_sum = k * (k - 1) // 2
        max_possible_sum = k * (19 - k) // 2  # k*(10-k+9)/2 = k*(19-k)/2
        
        if min_possible_sum <= s <= max_possible_sum:
            break
        k += 1
    
    # Now find the lexicographically smallest arrangement of k distinct digits summing to s
    digits = []
    used = [False] * 10
    remaining_sum = s
    
    for i in range(k):
        found = False
        # For first digit, we cannot use 0
        start_from = 0 if i == 0 else 1
        for d in range(start_from, 10):
            if used[d]:
                continue
                
            # Check if we can complete with remaining digits
            remaining_digits = k - 1 - i
            if remaining_digits == 0:
                if d == remaining_sum:
                    digits.append(d)
                    used[d] = True
                    found = True
                    break
            else:
                needed_sum = remaining_sum - d
                if can_achieve(needed_sum, remaining_digits):
                    digits.append(d)
                    used[d] = True
                    found = True
                    break
        
        if not found:
            break
    
    return int(''.join(map(str, digits)))

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = int(input())
    print(solve(s))
```

- For remaining_digits digits, largest digits are: 9, 8, ..., (10 - remaining_digits)

- Last term: 9  
- Number of terms: remaining_digits
- Sum = remaining_digits * (first + last) / 2 = remaining_digits * (10 - remaining_digits + 9) / 2 = remaining_digits * (19 - remaining_digits) / 2

- And for a fixed number of digits, the arrangement in ascending order gives the minimum number

Therefore, the algorithm is:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = int(input())
    digits = []
    # Start from 9 down to 1
    for d in range(9, 0, -1):
        if s >= d:
            digits.append(d)
            s -= d
            if s == 0:
                break
    # Sort digits in ascending order to form the minimum number
    digits.sort()
    # Convert to string and output
    print(''.join(map(str, digits)))
```

- s=8: s >= 8 → take 8 → s=0 → digits=[8] → 8 ✓  
- s=45: takes 9,8,7,6,5,4,3,2,1 → sorted → 1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9 → 123456789 ✓
- s=10: takes 9 → s=1 → takes 1 → s=0 → digits=[9,1] → sorted → [1,9] → 19 ✓

Perfect!

s=9 → takes 9 → output "9" ✓

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Find the minimum number with the given sum of digits $s$ such that all digits in it are distinct (i.e. all digits are unique).

For example, if $s=20$, then the answer is $389$. This is the minimum number in which all digits are different and the sum of the digits is $20$ ($3+8+9=20$).

For the given $s$ print the required number.


-----Input-----

The first line contains an integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 45$) — the number of test cases.

Each test case is specified by a line that contains the only integer $s$ ($1 \le s \le 45$).


-----Output-----

Print $t$ integers — the answers to the given test cases.


-----Examples-----

Input
4
20
8
45
10
Output
389
8
123456789
19

Therefore, the code:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = int(input())
    digits = []
    remaining = s
    # Go from 9 down to 0
    for digit in range(9, -1, -1):
        if digit <= remaining and digit not in digits:
            digits.append(digit)
            remaining -= digit
            # Early termination: if remaining is 0, we're done
            if remaining == 0:
                break
    # Sort digits in ascending order to get the minimum number
    digits.sort()
    # Convert to string and print
    print(''.join(map(str, digits)))
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Find the minimum number with the given sum of digits $s$ such that all digits in it are distinct (i.e. all digits are unique).

For example, if $s=20$, then the answer is $389$. This is the minimum number in which all digits are different and the sum of the digits is $20$ ($3+8+9=20$).

For the given $s$ print the required number.


-----Input-----

The first line contains an integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 45$) — the number of test cases.

Each test case is specified by a line that contains the only integer $s$ ($1 \le s \le 45$).


-----Output-----

Print $t$ integers — the answers to the given test cases.


-----Examples-----

Input
4
20
8
45
10
Output
389
8
123456789
19

## Algorithm

1. For each test case with sum s:

   - Sum from (10-k) to 9 = k * (10-k + 9) / 2 = k * (19-k) / 2
   - We need k*(19-k)/2 ≥ s

   k=2: 2*17/2=17 ✓
   k=3: 3*16/2=24 ✓
   k=4: 4*15/2=30 ✓
   
   So yes, we need k such that k*(19-k)/2 ≥ s
   
   Then, for this k, find k distinct digits from 1-9 that sum to s, arranged in ascending order, minimizing the overall number.

2. To find such digits:

   - We should try to minimize the digits from left to right
   - Start with digit 1, then 2, etc.
   - For each position, try smallest available digit such that the remaining sum can be achieved with the remaining positions

3. Actually, since we want the lexicographically smallest number, and digits are arranged in ascending order, we just need to find any k distinct digits from 1-9 that sum to s, and arrange them in ascending order.

4. But we need to ensure that such digits exist. Since the maximum sum with k digits is k*(19-k)/2 and we chose k such that this ≥ s, and the minimum sum with k distinct digits is 1+2+...+k = k(k+1)/2, we need to ensure s ≥ k(k+1)/2.

   Actually, if s is too large, we might not be able to form it. But by construction, for k satisfying k*(19-k)/2 ≥ s, we know the maximum is enough. The minimum sum is k(k+1)/2, so we need s ≥ k(k+1)/2.

   For example, s=45:

   So k=9.

   And the only way to get sum 45 with 9 distinct digits from 1-9 is to use all of them (sum = 45).

5. So the algorithm:
   - For each s, find the minimum k such that k*(19-k)/2 ≥ s
   - Then, find k distinct digits from 1-9 that sum to s
   - Arrange them in ascending order

6. How to find such digits efficiently?
   - We can greedily build from left to right
   - Start with the smallest possible digit at each position
   - For position i (0-indexed), try digit d from 1 upwards (ensuring d ≤ 9 and not already used)
   - Check if the remaining sum can be achieved with the remaining positions

   - But this is complex

   - Since we want the lexicographically smallest arrangement, we can try to construct it greedily from left to right
   - But we also need to ensure the sum can be achieved

   - Since the constraint is small (s ≤ 45, k ≤ 9), we can just try all combinations or use a greedy construction
   - Actually, we can use a greedy algorithm that tries to minimize each digit from left to right

But there's a more elegant approach:

We know that to get the lexicographically smallest number with distinct digits summing to s, we should:
1. Start with the smallest digits
2. The maximum sum we can get with the remaining positions should be ≥ remaining sum

So algorithm for constructing the digits:
- We have k positions to fill
- We need sum s
- For position 0, try digit d from 1 to 9:
  - d must be ≤ sum, and d ≥ 1
  - Remaining positions: k-1
  - Maximum sum we can achieve with remaining positions: sum of largest (k-1) digits available (excluding d)
  - But the largest (k-1) digits available are from (10-(k-1)) to 9 excluding d

Actually, it's simpler: the maximum sum with (k-1) distinct digits from the remaining (9 digits) is the sum of the largest (k-1) digits available.

But we don't know which digits are available.

Alternative: Since k is small (at most 9), and digits are from 1-9, we can use a greedy algorithm that tries digits from 1 upwards, and for each choice, checks if the remaining sum can be achieved with the remaining positions.

For the remaining positions, the maximum sum is the sum of the largest (remaining_pos) digits that are not used.

We can precompute the maximum possible sum for any number of positions with any set of unused digits.

But since we're going to implement this, let me code a simple constructive algorithm:

```python
def solve(s):
    # Find minimum k such that k*(19-k)//2 >= s
    k = 1
    while k*(19-k)//2 < s:
        k += 1
    
    # Now find k distinct digits from 1-9 that sum to s
    # Use greedy approach: try to assign smallest possible digit at each position
    digits = []
    remaining_sum = s
    available = set(range(1, 10))  # digits 1-9
    
    for pos in range(k):
        # Try each digit from 1 to 9 (in order), but not already used
        found = False
        for d in range(1, 10):
            if d not in available:
                continue
            # Can we achieve the remaining_sum with remaining positions?
            remaining_positions = k - pos - 1
            if remaining_positions == 0:
                if remaining_sum == 0:
                    digits.append(d)
                    found = True
                    break
                else:
                    continue
            # Else, check if remaining_sum can be achieved with remaining_positions and available digits
            # Maximum sum with remaining_positions from remaining available digits
            # We need to compute the sum of the largest remaining_positions digits in available
            available_list = sorted(available, reverse=True)
            max_possible_sum = sum(available_list[:remaining_positions])
            if max_possible_sum >= remaining_sum:
                digits.append(d)
                available.remove(d)
                remaining_sum -= d
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            # This should not happen given our k choice
            break
    
    return ''.join(map(str, digits))
```

- digits=[1], available={2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9}, remaining_sum=44
- pos=1: d=2: remaining_sum=42, remaining_pos=7
- max sum with 7 digits = 9+8+7+6+5+4+3 = 42 ≥ 42 → yes
- digits=[1,2]
- Continue this way, we'll get [1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8,9] → 123456789 ✓

s=10:
- k=2: 2*17//2=17 ≥ 10 → k=2
- pos=0: d=1: remaining_sum=9, remaining_pos=1
- max sum with 1 digit = 9 ≥ 9 → yes
- digits=[1], remaining_sum=9
- pos=1: d=9: remaining_sum=0 → yes
- digits=[1,9] → 19 ✓

Perfect!

However, there's a potential issue: when we choose d=1 at pos=0, the remaining sum is 9, and we need one digit that is 9. But what if we could also get 2+7=9 or 3+6=9, etc.? But we want the lexicographically smallest number, and 19 < 28 (since 19 < 28), so 19 is better.

Our algorithm picks the smallest possible d at each position that allows completion, which gives the lexicographically smallest number. ✓

One more test: s=1
- k=1: 1*18//2=9 ≥ 1 → k=1
- d=1: remaining_sum=0, remaining_pos=0 → yes → answer=1 ✓

Edge case: s=9 → k=1, answer=9

All seem correct.

Let me implement:

```python
def solve(s):
    # Find minimum k such that k*(19-k)//2 >= s
    k = 1
    while k * (19 - k) // 2 < s:
        k += 1
    
    # Find k distinct digits from 1-9 that sum to s
    digits = []
    remaining_sum = s
    available = set(range(1, 10))
    
    for pos in range(k):
        found = False
        for d in range(1, 10):
            if d not in available:
                continue
            # Check if we can achieve remaining_sum with remaining positions
            remaining_positions = k - pos - 1
            if remaining_positions == 0:
                if remaining_sum == 0:
                    digits.append(d)
                    found = True
                    break
                else:
                    continue
            # Otherwise, check max possible sum with remaining_positions from available digits
            # Convert available to sorted list in descending order
            sorted_available = sorted(available, reverse=True)
            max_possible_sum = sum(sorted_available[:remaining_positions])
            if max_possible_sum >= remaining_sum:
                digits.append(d)
                available.remove(d)
                remaining_sum -= d
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            # Should not happen given the constraints
            break
    
    return ''.join(map(str, digits))

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = int(input())
    print(solve(s))
```

  - max sum with 2 digits = 9+8=17 ≥ 17 → yes
  - digits=[1], remaining_sum=17

  - digits=[1,8], available={2,3,4,5,6,7,9}, remaining_sum=9

289? 2+8+9=19 > 18
So 189 seems smallest.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = int(input())
```
    digits = []
```python
    current_sum = 0
    # Go from 9 down to 1
    for digit in range(9, 0, -1):
        if current_sum + digit <= s:
            digits.append(digit)
            current_sum += digit
            # Early termination if we've reached exactly s
            if current_sum == s:
                break
    
    # Sort digits in ascending order to form the minimum number
    digits.sort()
    print(''.join(map(str, digits)))
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if we can select exactly `x` elements from an array such that their sum is odd.

Key insights about odd sums:
- The sum of numbers is odd when there's an odd number of odd elements in the sum
- Even numbers don't affect the parity (odd/even nature) of the sum
- Only odd numbers contribute to whether the sum is odd or even

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **Odd sum condition**: A sum is odd if and only if there's an odd number of odd elements in the selected subset.

2. **Strategy**:
   - Count how many odd numbers we have in the array
   - We need to select exactly `x` elements with an odd number of odd elements
   - The remaining `x - (number of odd elements selected)` elements must be even

3. **Cases to consider**:
   - If we have `odd_count` odd numbers in the array
   - We can select 0, 1, 2, ..., up to `odd_count` odd numbers
   - The sum will be odd when we select an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...)

4. **Feasibility conditions**:
   - We need at least one odd number to make the sum odd (if we select 0 odd numbers, sum is even)
   - We need to be able to select an odd number of odd elements from the available odd elements
   - We also need to have enough even elements to fill the remaining positions

5. **Edge cases**:
   - If we need to select 0 elements (not applicable here since x ≥ 1)
   - If we have no odd numbers at all, we cannot make an odd sum
   - If we have at least one odd number, we can potentially make an odd sum

## Solution Logic

For a sum to be odd when selecting exactly `x` elements:
1. We need at least one odd number in the array
2. We need to be able to select an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...) such that:
   - The number of odd elements selected ≤ total odd elements available
   - The number of odd elements selected is odd
   - The remaining (x - odd_count_selected) elements must be selected from even elements
   - We must have at least (x - odd_count_selected) even elements available

## Implementation

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even numbers
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 1:
            odd_count += 1
        else:
            even_count += 1
    
    # If we have no odd numbers, any sum will be even
    if odd_count == 0:
        print("No")
        continue
    
    # We need to select exactly x elements with odd sum
    # This is possible if we can select an odd number of odd elements
    # such that we have enough even elements to fill the rest
    
    possible = False
    
    # Try selecting 1, 3, 5, ... odd numbers (odd counts)
    # for k in range(1, odd_count + 1, 2):  # k is odd number of odd elements
    #   remaining_even = x - k
    #   if remaining_even >= 0 and remaining_even <= even_count:
    #       possible = True
    #       break
    
    # We can select an odd number of odd elements if:
    # 1. We have at least one odd element (we do)
    # 2. We can select k odd elements where k is odd and k >= 1
    # 3. We have enough even elements to fill the rest (x - k)
    
    # We can achieve an odd sum if there exists at least one odd k in [1, odd_count] 
    # such that x - k >= 0 and x - k <= even_count
    
    for k in range(1, odd_count + 1, 2):  # k is odd: 1, 3, 5, ...
        remaining_even = x - k
        if remaining_even >= 0 and remaining_even <= even_count:
            possible = True
            break
    
    print("Yes" if possible else "No")
```

Let me verify with the examples:

1. `[999]`, x=1: odd_count=1, even_count=0
   - Try k=1: remaining_even = 1-1 = 0, which is >=0 and <=0 ✓
   - Answer: Yes ✓

2. `[1000]`, x=1: odd_count=0, even_count=1
   - odd_count=0, so print "No" ✓

3. `[51,50]`, x=1: odd_count=1, even_count=1
   - Try k=1: remaining_even = 1-1 = 0, which is >=0 and <=1 ✓
   - Answer: Yes ✓

4. `[51,50]`, x=2: odd_count=1, even_count=1
   - Try k=1: remaining_even = 2-1 = 1, which is >=0 and <=1 ✓
   - Answer: Yes ✓

5. `[101,102,103]`, x=3: odd_count=2, even_count=1
   - Try k=1: remaining_even = 3-1 = 2, but even_count=1, so 2 > 1 ✗
   - Try k=3: k=3 > 2 (odd_count), so doesn't work
   - No valid k found → Answer: No ✓

Perfect! The solution works for all test cases.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even numbers
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 1:
            odd_count += 1
        else:
            even_count += 1
    
    # If we have no odd numbers, any sum will be even
    if odd_count == 0:
        print("No")
        continue
    
    # We need to select exactly x elements with odd sum
    # This is possible if we can select an odd number of odd elements
    # such that we have enough even elements to fill the rest
    
    possible = False
    
    # Try selecting 1, 3, 5, ... odd numbers (odd counts)
    for k in range(1, odd_count + 1, 2):  # k is odd: 1, 3, 5, ...
        remaining_even = x - k
        if remaining_even >= 0 and remaining_even <= even_count:
            possible = True
            break
    
    print("Yes" if possible else "No")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if we can select exactly `x` elements from an array such that their sum is odd.

Key insights:
- A sum is odd if and only if it has an odd number of odd elements
- Even + even = even
- Odd + odd = even  
- Even + odd = odd
- So the sum of numbers is odd when we have an odd count of odd numbers

## Analysis

For the sum to be odd, we need exactly an odd number of odd elements in our selection of `x` elements.

Let me think about the possible cases:

1. **Count of odd numbers in the array**: Let's call this `odd_count`
2. **We need to select exactly `x` elements** with an odd number of odd elements

The key insight is that we can choose any combination of elements, so we just need to check if it's possible to have an odd number of odd elements in our selection of `x` elements.

Cases to consider:
- If we have 0 odd numbers in the array: we can only get even sums, so answer is "No"
- If we have at least one odd number, we can form odd sums if we can select an odd number of odd elements

But we also need to be able to select exactly `x` elements total.

So the question becomes: can we select an odd number of odd elements (say `k`, where `k` is odd) such that `k ≤ odd_count` and `x - k ≤ even_count` (where `even_count = n - odd_count`)?

Actually, let me reconsider this more systematically:

We need to pick exactly `x` elements with an odd total sum.
- The sum is odd iff we have an odd number of odd elements in our selection.
- We can choose any number of odd elements from 0 to `odd_count`, as long as we have enough even elements to make up the rest.

So we need to check if there exists some odd number `k` (where `k` is odd and `1 ≤ k ≤ odd_count`) such that:
- We select `k` odd elements (k must be odd)
- We select `x - k` even elements
- We need `x - k ≥ 0` and `x - k ≤ even_count`

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Count the number of odd elements in the array
2. Count the number of even elements in the array
3. We need to form a selection of `x` elements with an odd sum
4. This is possible if and only if:
   - There exists an odd number `k` (1, 3, 5, ...) such that:
     - `k ≤ odd_count` (we don't have enough odd elements)
     - `x - k ≥ 0` (we don't need more elements than we have)
     - `x - k ≤ even_count` (we don't have enough even elements)

Special cases:
- If `odd_count = 0`: we can only get even sums, so return "No"
- If `odd_count > 0`: we need to check if we can have an odd number of odd elements in our selection

But we can simplify: we just need to check if we can have at least one odd number of odd elements in our selection of `x` elements.

We can have an odd number of odd elements if:
- There exists an odd number `k` where `k ≤ odd_count` and `x - k ≤ even_count` and `x - k ≥ 0`

This is equivalent to:
- `odd_count > 0` AND (there exists an odd k such that k ≤ odd_count and x - k ≤ even_count and x - k ≥ 0)

But we can simplify further:
- We need at least one odd number of odd elements that we can select
- The smallest odd number we can select is 1
- So we need `1 ≤ odd_count` and `x - 1 ≤ even_count` and `x - 1 ≥ 0`
- But wait, what if we can select more than 1 odd element? For example, we might need to select 3 odd elements if we have only 2 even elements.

Actually, we just need to check if there's ANY odd k that works.

Since we want the simplest condition, let's think about the constraints:
- We need an odd number of odd elements
- The minimum odd count we can have is 1
- The maximum odd count we can have is the total odd_count (if odd_count is odd, we can use odd_count; if odd_count is even, we can use odd_count-1)

So we need to check if there's an odd k such that:
1. `k ≤ odd_count`
2. `x - k ≥ 0` 
3. `x - k ≤ even_count`

Since we want existence, we can check if:
- `odd_count > 0` AND (there exists odd k where k ≤ odd_count and x - k ≤ even_count and x - k ≥ 0)

We can simplify: the smallest possible odd number is 1. If we can use k=1, that's good. 
But what if k=1 is impossible? Then we try k=3, then k=5, etc.

Actually, let's check if k=1 works: we need `1 ≤ odd_count` and `x - 1 ≤ even_count` and `x - 1 ≥ 0`.

But what if k=1 doesn't work due to lack of even elements, but k=3 might work?

For example: n=4, x=4, array = [1,1,2,2] (odd_count=2, even_count=2)
We want to select 4 elements. 
- k=1: need 1 odd, 3 even → we have only 2 even → impossible
- k=3: need 3 odd, 1 even → we have only 2 odd → impossible
So we cannot make odd sum.

But the condition should be: we need an odd number of odd elements that is ≤ odd_count and we need x-k ≤ even_count.

So the algorithm:
1. Count odd_count and even_count
2. If odd_count == 0: return "No" (no way to get odd sum)
3. Otherwise, check if there exists any odd number k such that:
   - k ≤ odd_count
   - x - k ≥ 0  
   - x - k ≤ even_count
4. If such k exists, return "Yes", else "No"

But since k must be odd, we can check odd values: 1, 3, 5, ... up to odd_count.

Actually, we can simplify: we need to check if we can have an odd number of odd elements in our selection.

The minimum number of odd elements we need is 1, maximum is odd_count (if odd_count is odd) or odd_count-1 (if odd_count is even).

But we just need to know if there's at least one possibility.

We can check: is there an odd k in the range [1, min(odd_count, x)] such that x-k ≤ even_count?

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=1, x=1, a=[999]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 0
- k=1: 1 ≤ 1, 1-1=0 ≤ 0 → yes → "Yes"

Example 2: n=1, x=1, a=[1000]
- odd_count = 0 → "No"

Example 3: n=2, x=1, a=[51,50]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 1
- k=1: 1 ≤ 1, 1-1=0 ≤ 1 → yes → "Yes"

Example 4: n=2, x=2, a=[51,50]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 1
- k=1: need 1 odd, 1 even → 1 ≤ 1, 2-1=1 ≤ 1 → yes → "Yes"

Example 5: n=3, x=3, a=[101,102,103]
- odd_count = 2, even_count = 1
- k=1: need 1 odd, 2 even → 2 even needed, but we have only 1 → no
- k=3: need 3 odd, but only 2 odd available → no
- So "No"

This matches the expected output.

So the algorithm is:
For each test case:
1. Read n, x and array
2. Count odd_count (number of odd elements) and even_count (number of even elements)
3. If odd_count == 0: print "No"
4. Else: check if there exists an odd integer k such that:
   - k >= 1
   - k <= odd_count
   - k <= x (so x-k >= 0)
   - x - k <= even_count (so we have enough even elements)
5. If such k exists, print "Yes", else "No"

We can implement this efficiently by checking odd values from 1 upward until we find one that works.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even elements
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 0:
            even_count += 1
        else:
            odd_count += 1
    
    # If there are no odd numbers, we cannot get an odd sum
    if odd_count == 0:
        print("No")
        continue
    
    # Check if there exists an odd number k such that:
    # k <= odd_count, k <= x, and x - k <= even_count
    found = False
    # Check odd values: 1, 3, 5, ...
    k = 1
    while k <= odd_count and k <= x:
        if x - k <= even_count:
            found = True
            break
        k += 2  # next odd number
    
    if found:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if we can select exactly `x` elements from an array such that their sum is odd.

Key insights:
- A sum is odd if and only if it contains an odd number of odd elements (0 odd elements = even sum, 1 odd element = odd sum, 2 odd elements = even sum, etc.)
- So we need exactly an odd number of odd elements in our selection

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze when a sum can be odd
- The sum of numbers is odd if and only if the count of odd numbers in the sum is odd
- So we need to select exactly an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...) from our selection of `x` elements

### Step 2: Determine constraints
- We need to select exactly `x` elements
- Among these `x` elements, we need an odd number of odd elements
- The remaining elements (x - odd_count) must be even elements

### Step 3: Key observations
1. If we have no odd numbers in the array, we cannot make an odd sum (all elements are even)
2. If we have at least one odd number, we might be able to make an odd sum
3. We need to check if we can form a selection with an odd count of odd numbers

### Step 4: Algorithm
1. Count how many odd numbers are in the array
2. Count how many even numbers are in the array
3. We need to select exactly `x` elements with an odd number of odd elements
4. For this to be possible:
   - We must have at least one odd number available (otherwise sum is always even)
   - We need to be able to pick an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...) such that the total count becomes `x`

### Step 5: Detailed conditions
- If there are no odd numbers in the array → impossible → "No"
- If there is at least one odd number:
  - We can pick 1 odd element if we have at least 1 odd and have enough room to pick the rest from even elements
  - We can pick 3 odd elements if we have at least 3 odd and enough even elements to fill the rest
  - And so on...

Actually, let me think simpler:

We need to check if we can form a selection of `x` elements such that the count of odd numbers among them is odd.

This is possible if and only if:
1. There is at least one odd number in the array
2. We can actually pick an odd number of odd elements (from the available odd elements) and the rest from even elements such that total count is exactly `x`

So:
- Let `odd_count` = number of odd elements in array
- Let `even_count` = number of even elements in array

We can pick `k` odd elements (where `k` is odd) and `(x - k)` even elements, where:
- `1 ≤ k ≤ odd_count` and `k` is odd
- `0 ≤ x - k ≤ even_count` (we can pick at most `even_count` even elements)

So the question becomes: does there exist an odd integer `k` such that:
1. `k ≥ 1` and `k ≤ odd_count`
2. `x - k ≥ 0` and `x - k ≤ even_count`

### Step 6: Implementation

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even numbers
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 0:
            even_count += 1
        else:
            odd_count += 1
    
    # If there are no odd numbers, we cannot make an odd sum
    if odd_count == 0:
        print("No")
        continue
    
    # Check if we can pick an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...)
    # We need to find if there exists an odd k such that:
    # 1 <= k <= odd_count
    # 0 <= x - k <= even_count
    
    possible = False
    # Try all odd values of k from 1 to odd_count
    for k in range(1, odd_count + 1, 2):  # k = 1, 3, 5, ...
        remaining = x - k
        if remaining >= 0 and remaining <= even_count:
            possible = True
            break
    
    print("Yes" if possible else "No")
```

- Try k=3: k=3 > odd_count=2 → invalid
- No valid k → "No" ✓

   - The sum is odd if and only if we have an odd number of odd elements in our selection
   - This means we can have 1, 3, 5, ... odd elements in total

2. **What determines the parity of each element?**
   - If an element is even, it doesn't affect the parity of the sum
   - If an element is odd, it flips the parity of the sum

3. **Key observations:**
   - We need exactly `x` elements total
   - Among these `x` elements, we need an odd number of odd elements
   - So we can have 1, 3, 5, ... odd elements (all odd numbers)

4. **Edge cases:**
   - If we have 0 odd elements in the array, we cannot make an odd sum (all selections will be even)
   - If we have at least one odd element, we might be able to form an odd sum

5. **When is it impossible?**
   - If there are no odd elements in the array, then any selection will have an even sum
   - If we have odd elements, but we cannot form a valid count of odd elements needed in our selection

## Algorithm Development

Let's think about when we can form an odd sum with exactly `x` elements:

1. Count the total number of odd elements in the array
2. If there are 0 odd elements, then no matter what we select, the sum will be even → answer is "No"
3. If there is at least one odd element, we need to check if we can select an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...) such that the total count is exactly `x`

The critical insight: we can form an odd sum with exactly `x` elements if and only if:
- There is at least one odd element in the array, AND
- We can choose an odd number of odd elements from the available odd elements

But we also need to ensure we have enough odd elements to form at least one odd count.

Actually, simpler approach:
- If there are 0 odd elements → impossible ("No")
- If there are at least one odd element, we need to check if we can form an odd sum with exactly `x` elements

To form an odd sum with exactly `x` elements:
- We need to choose an odd number of odd elements (k), where 1 ≤ k ≤ min(x, count_of_odd_elements)
- And we need to choose (x - k) even elements

This is possible if:
- There is at least one odd element (so k ≥ 1)
- We have at least one odd element available (we have at least one odd element in array)
- We can choose k odd elements where k is odd and k ≤ count_of_odd_elements
- We can choose (x - k) even elements, which requires that (x - k) ≥ 0 and (x - k) ≤ count_of_even_elements

Wait, but we don't actually need to know the exact count of even elements - we just need to know if we can form such a selection.

Actually, since we can choose any elements, and we just need to check if it's possible:

If there's at least one odd element in the array, and we have at least one odd element available, then:
- We can always choose exactly 1 odd element (the smallest odd count that makes sum odd)
- We need to check if we can choose 1 odd element and (x-1) even elements

For this to be possible:
- We must have at least 1 odd element available
- We must have at least (x-1) even elements available (if x-1 > 0)

But what if x = 1? Then we need exactly 1 element. We need at least one odd element.

What if x > 1? Then we need both at least one odd element and at least (x-1) even elements.

Wait, let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: n=1, x=1, array=[999]
- One odd element, x=1 → select 1 element which is odd → sum is odd → "Yes"

Example 2: n=1, x=1, array=[1000]
- One even element, x=1 → select 1 element which is even → sum is even → "No"

Example 3: n=2, x=1, array=[51, 50]
- 51 is odd, 50 is even
- We can select 51 (1 odd element) → sum is odd → "Yes"

Example 4: n=2, x=2, array=[51, 50]
- We need 2 elements: 51 (odd) and 50 (even)
- Sum = odd + even = odd → "Yes"

Example 5: n=3, x=3, array=[101, 102, 103]
- 101 and 103 are odd (2 odd elements), 102 is even
- We need to select all 3 elements
- Sum = odd + even + odd = even + odd = odd? Wait: odd + even = odd, odd + odd = even
- So odd + even + odd = (odd + even) + odd = odd + odd = even → sum is even → "No"

So in example 5, even though there are odd elements, the sum is even because we have 2 odd elements (even count).

This confirms: sum is odd iff number of odd elements selected is odd.

So the algorithm:

1. Count the number of odd elements in the array
2. If count_odd == 0:
   - We cannot form an odd sum → "No"
3. Otherwise:
   - We want to select exactly x elements with an odd number of odd elements
   - Let k be the number of odd elements we select (k must be odd, 1 ≤ k ≤ min(x, count_odd))
   - We need to select (x - k) even elements
   - For this to be possible, we need:
     - k ≥ 1 (odd)
     - k ≤ count_odd
     - x - k ≥ 0 (so k ≤ x)
     - x - k ≤ count_even (where count_even = n - count_odd)

But we can simplify: since we just need to know if it's possible, we can ask:
- Is there an odd number k such that 1 ≤ k ≤ min(x, count_odd) and (x - k) ≤ (n - count_odd)?

Actually, since we can choose any elements, and we just need to know if such a k exists, we can think about constraints.

Alternative insight: 
- If we have at least one odd element, we can always try to make the sum odd by selecting 1 odd element and the rest even elements (if possible)
- So the condition is: 
  - There's at least one odd element, AND
  - We can form a selection with an odd number of odd elements

For a selection of x elements to have an odd number of odd elements, we need an odd k where k ≥ 1, k ≤ min(x, count_odd), and (x - k) ≤ (n - count_odd)

The minimal odd k is 1. So if we can have k=1, that gives us an odd sum.

We can have k=1 if:
- 1 ≤ min(x, count_odd) → which means x ≥ 1 and count_odd ≥ 1 (which we already have)
- (x - 1) ≤ (n - count_odd) → x - 1 ≤ n - count_odd → x ≤ n - count_odd + 1

But wait, this is only for k=1. What about other odd k values? For example, k=3,5,...?

But we want the simplest condition. Let me think of when it's impossible.

- If we have no choice: for example, if x=1 and there are no odd elements (already handled)
- Or if x is large and we don't have enough even elements to pair with odd elements

Actually, if we have at least one odd element, we can always try to select exactly 1 odd element and (x-1) even elements, provided:
- x-1 ≥ 0 (x ≥ 1, which is true)
- x-1 ≤ count_even = n - count_odd

- count_odd > 0 and (x - 1) ≤ (n - count_odd)

Actually, the problem is simpler than I thought.

1. Count odd elements in array

   - How to check? The smallest odd number ≥ lower_bound
   - If there's an odd number in [lower_bound, upper_bound], return "Yes", else "No"

  - If l is odd, then we have an odd number

  - So answer is: (l ≤ r) and (l is odd or (l+1 ≤ r and l+1 is odd))

If l is even, we need l+1 ≤ r

Yes.

Actually, this is equivalent to: l <= r and (l % 2 == 1 or l+1 <= r)

But l+1 <= r means the odd number l+1 is in range.

Actually, if l <= r, then:
- The odd numbers in [l,r] exist if and only if (l % 2 == 1) or (r - l >= 1 and (l+1) % 2 == 1)

But (l+1) % 2 == 1 when l+1 is odd, i.e., when l is even.

So yes: if l <= r and (l is odd or l+1 <= r), then there's an odd number in [l,r].

But wait: if l is odd and l <= r, then l is an odd number in the range → good
If l is even and l+1 <= r, then l+1 is an odd number and l+1 >= l and l+1 <= r → good
If l is even and l+1 > r, then the next odd number is l+1, which is > r → no odd number in [l,r]

Perfect.

So final algorithm:

For each test case:
1. Read n, x
2. Read array
3. Count odd elements (count_odd)
4. If count_odd == 0:
   - Print "No"
5. Else:
   - l = max(1, x - (n - count_odd))
   - r = min(x, count_odd)
   - If l <= r and (l % 2 == 1 or (l + 1 <= r)):
     - Print "Yes"
   - Else:
     - Print "No"

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=1, x=1, array=[999]
- count_odd = 1
- l = max(1, 1 - (1-1)) = max(1, 1-0) = 1
- r = min(1, 1) = 1
- l <= r: 1<=1 true
- l%2 == 1: 1%2 == 1 → true → "Yes" ✓

Example 2: n=1, x=1, array=[1000]
- count_odd = 0 → "No" ✓

Example 3: n=2, x=1, array=[51,50]
- count_odd = 1 (51 is odd)
- l = max(1, 1 - (2-1)) = max(1, 1-1) = max(1,0) = 1
- r = min(1,1) = 1
- 1<=1 true, 1%2==1 → true → "Yes" ✓

Example 4: n=2, x=2, array=[51,50]
- count_odd = 1
- l = max(1, 2 - (2-1)) = max(1, 2-1) = max(1,1) = 1
- r = min(2,1) = 1
- 1<=1 true, 1%2==1 → true → "Yes" ✓

Example 5: n=3, x=3, array=[101,102,103]
- 101,103 are odd → count_odd = 2
- l = max(1, 3 - (3-2)) = max(1, 3-1) = max(1,2) = 2
- r = min(3,2) = 2
- l <= r: 2<=2 true
- l%2 == 1? 2%2 == 0 → false
- l+1 <= r? 3 <= 2? false → so condition false → "No" ✓

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd elements
    count_odd = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 1:
            count_odd += 1
    
    # If no odd elements, cannot form odd sum
    if count_odd == 0:
        print("No")
    else:
        # l is the minimum possible k (number of odd elements)
        # l >= 1 (we need at least one odd element)
        # l >= x - (n - count_odd) (we need at least (x - l) even elements)
        l = max(1, x - (n - count_odd))
        # r is the maximum possible k (number of odd elements)
        # r <= x (we can't select more than x elements)
        # r <= count_odd (we don't have that many odd elements)
        r = min(x, count_odd)
        
        # Check if there exists an odd k in [l, r]
        if l <= r and (l % 2 == 1 or (l + 1 <= r)):
            print("Yes")
        else:
            print("No")
```

for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd elements
    count_odd = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 1:
            count_odd += 1
    
    # If no odd elements, cannot form odd sum
    if count_odd == 0:
        print("No")
    else:
        # l is the minimum possible k (number of odd elements)

        # l >= x - (n - count_odd) (we need at least (x - l) even elements)
        l = max(1, x - (n - count_odd))
        # r is the maximum possible k (number of odd elements)
        # r <= x (we can't select more than x elements)
        # r <= count_odd (we don't have that many odd elements)

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ $(1 \le x \le n \le 1000)$ — the length of the array and the number of elements you need to choose.

The next line of each test case contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \dots, a_n$ $(1 \le a_i \le 1000)$ — elements of the array.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if we can select exactly `x` elements from an array such that their sum is odd.

Key insights about odd/even sums:
- Even + Even = Even
- Odd + Odd = Even  
- Even + Odd = Odd
- Even + Even + Even = Even
- Odd + Odd + Even = Even
- Odd + Odd + Odd = Odd
- Odd + Odd + Odd + Odd = Even

For a sum to be odd, we need an odd number of odd elements in our selection.

## Analysis

Let me think about when we can get an odd sum with exactly `x` elements:

1. **Odd sum condition**: The sum is odd if and only if the count of selected odd numbers is odd (1, 3, 5, ...)

2. **Cases to consider**:
   - If we have 0 odd numbers in the array: we can only make even sums, so answer is "No"
   - If we have at least one odd number: we can potentially make odd sums

3. **Key observations**:
   - We need to select an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...)
   - We also need to select exactly `x` elements in total
   - The remaining elements (to make up `x` total) will be even elements

4. **Possible scenarios**:
   - If we have fewer than `x` odd elements, we can only use all available odd elements and the rest must be even
   - We need to check if we can select an odd number of odd elements such that the total count is exactly `x`

5. **Cases when it's impossible**:
   - If we have 0 odd numbers and `x >= 1`, we can never get an odd sum
   - If we have no choice (like in the examples)

## Step-by-step solution:

1. Count the total number of odd numbers in the array
2. Check if we can select an odd number of odd elements such that the total count is exactly `x`
3. We can select 1, 3, 5, ... odd elements (must be odd count)
4. For a selection of `k` odd elements (where `k` is odd), we need `x - k` even elements
5. We must have `x - k >= 0` and `x - k <= available_even_elements`
6. We must have `k <= available_odd_elements`

## Algorithm:

1. Count odd and even elements in the array
2. If there are 0 odd elements, answer is "No" (since we can't get odd sum)
3. Otherwise, check if we can select an odd number of odd elements such that:
   - The number of odd elements selected (`k`) is odd
   - `k` ranges from 1 to `min(x, available_odd_elements)`
   - `x - k` (number of even elements needed) is >= 0 and <= available_even_elements

Example 4: n=2, x=2, [51,50] → must select both → 1 odd + 1 even → odd+even=odd → "Yes"

   - So impossible → "No"

The key insight: We need an odd number of odd elements, and we need enough even elements to fill the rest.

So the algorithm:
1. Count odd and even elements
2. If odd_count == 0: return "No"

```python
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even elements
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 0:
            even_count += 1
        else:
            odd_count += 1
    
    # If there are no odd numbers, we can never get an odd sum
    if odd_count == 0:
        print("No")
    else:
        # Try all possible odd numbers of odd elements to select (1, 3, 5, ...)
        found = False
        # k is the number of odd elements we select (must be odd)
        k = 1
        while k <= min(x, odd_count):
            # We need to select (x - k) even elements
            even_needed = x - k
            if even_needed >= 0 and even_needed <= even_count:
                found = True
                break
            k += 2  # Next odd number
        
        if found:
            print("Yes")
        else:
            print("No")
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if we can select exactly `x` elements from an array such that their sum is odd.

Key insights:
- A sum is odd if and only if there's an odd number of odd elements in the sum
- The sum of even numbers is always even
- The sum of odd numbers is odd when we have an odd count of odd numbers
- We need exactly `x` elements in total

## Analysis

Let me think about when we can get an odd sum with exactly `x` elements:

1. **Case 1: We need an odd number of odd elements in our selected x elements**
2. **Case 2: We can only achieve an odd sum if we have at least one odd number available**

The sum will be odd if and only if the total count of odd numbers among the selected elements is odd.

So we need to check:
- Do we have enough odd numbers to form an odd count when selecting exactly x elements?
- Can we arrange the selection to get an odd number of odd elements?

Let me break it down:
- Count the total number of odd elements in the array
- We need to select `x` elements such that the number of odd elements among them is odd

The possible distributions:
- We can select 1, 3, 5, ... odd elements (odd counts)
- We can select 0, 2, 4, ... even elements (even counts)

For a sum to be odd, we need an odd count of odd elements.

## Conditions for feasibility:

1. **Minimum odd elements needed**: We need at least 1 odd element to have any chance of getting an odd sum
2. **Maximum odd elements available**: We can take at most `min(x, count_odd)` odd elements
3. **Can we form an odd count?**: We can form an odd count if:
   - We can select at least 1 odd element AND at least one odd count between 1 and the maximum possible odd elements

Actually, the key insight is simpler:
- If we have no odd elements in the array, then any selection will have only even elements, so the sum will always be even → answer is "No"
- If we have at least one odd element, we need to check if we can form an odd sum with exactly x elements

Now, can we always form an odd sum when we have at least one odd element?

It depends on whether we can choose an odd number of odd elements from the available odd elements.

We need to check if there exists an odd number k such that:
- k ≤ total_odd_elements (we can pick k odd elements)
- k ≥ 1 (we need at least one odd element)
- k ≤ x (we can pick at most x elements total, so at least x-k even elements needed)

Wait, let me reconsider: we need exactly x elements, with an odd count of odd elements.

For a valid selection:
- We pick `k` odd elements where k is odd (1, 3, 5, ...)
- We pick `x - k` even elements
- We need `k ≤ total_odd_elements` (enough odd elements available)
- We need `x - k ≥ 0` (non-negative even elements)
- We need `x - k ≤ total_even_elements` (enough even elements available)

But actually, since we just need to know if it's possible, we can simplify:

When do we NOT have a solution?
- When we have no odd elements at all → sum is always even
- When the only way to get an odd sum requires more odd elements than available, or more even elements than available

But there's a simpler pattern:

**Key insight**: 
- If there are no odd numbers in the array → impossible to get odd sum
- If there is at least one odd number, we can usually arrange to get an odd sum, but we need to check feasibility

Actually, let me think of edge cases:
- x = 1: we can pick any single element. If there's at least one odd element, we can pick it → sum is odd
- x ≥ 2: we need to pick an odd number of odd elements

So the condition is:
1. If there are no odd elements → "No"
2. If there is at least one odd element:
   - If x = 1 → we can just pick an odd element → "Yes"
   - If x ≥ 2 → we need to check if we can pick an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...) such that:
     - We pick at least 1 odd element
     - We pick at most the available odd elements
     - We pick at most the available even elements for the remaining (x - k) positions

But actually, since we can always pick 1 odd element and (x-1) even elements (if available), the only constraint is:
- We need at least 1 odd element available
- We need at least (x-1) even elements available (if we want to pick 1 odd element)

Wait, that's not right. We might not need (x-1) even elements. We could pick more odd elements.

The minimum requirement is:
- We need to pick an odd number of odd elements: at least 1
- We need at least 1 odd element available
- We need to pick (total_selected - odd_count) even elements, which must be ≤ total_even_elements

The most flexible case is picking exactly 1 odd element and (x-1) even elements.

So for x ≥ 2, we can achieve an odd sum if:
- We have at least one odd element 
- We have at least (x-1) even elements (to pick x-1 even elements with 1 odd element)

But what if we have fewer even elements? Can we pick more odd elements?

For example, if we have 3 odd elements and 1 even element, and x = 4:
- Option 1: 1 odd + 3 even → need 3 even elements, but only have 1 → impossible
- Option 2: 3 odd + 1 even → sum = 3+1 = 4 odd elements → sum is odd (since 3 is odd)
- This works! We need exactly 3 odd elements and 1 even element

So we can pick any odd number of odd elements k, where:
- k is odd, 1 ≤ k ≤ min(x, total_odd)
- x - k ≥ 0 → k ≤ x
- x - k ≤ total_even → k ≥ x - total_even

For a valid solution to exist, there must exist an odd k satisfying:
- k ≥ max(1, x - total_even) 
- k ≤ min(x, total_odd)
- k is odd

Therefore, the answer is "Yes" if and only if there exists an odd integer k in the range [max(1, x - total_even), min(x, total_odd)].

But let's check if this can be simplified.

Actually, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: n=1, x=1, array=[999]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 0
- Need k odd such that k ≥ max(1, 1-0)=1, k ≤ min(1,1)=1
- k=1 is odd → Yes ✓

Example 2: n=1, x=1, array=[1000]
- odd_count = 0 → no odd elements → No ✓

Example 3: n=2, x=1, array=[51,50]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 1
- Need k ≥ max(1,1-1)=1, k ≤ min(1,1)=1
- k=1 is odd → Yes ✓

Example 4: n=2, x=2, array=[51,50]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 1
- Need k ≥ max(1,2-1)=1, k ≤ min(2,1)=1
- k=1 is odd → Yes ✓

Example 5: n=3, x=3, array=[101,102,103]
- odd_count = 2 (101,103), even_count = 1 (102)
- Need k ≥ max(1,3-1)=2, k ≤ min(3,2)=2
- k must be odd and ≥2 → k=3? But 3 > 2 → no such odd k → No ✓

This matches!

So the algorithm:
1. Count total odd and even elements
2. If odd_count == 0: return "No"
3. Find the range of possible odd counts k: 
   - Lower bound: max(1, x - even_count)
   - Upper bound: min(x, odd_count)
4. Check if there exists any odd integer k in [lower_bound, upper_bound]
5. If yes → "Yes", else "No"

Let me verify with a simple case: x=3, odd_count=1, even_count=2
- lower_bound = max(1,3-2)=1
- upper_bound = min(3,1)=1
- k=1 is odd → Yes

But with x=3, odd_count=1, even_count=1 (only 1 even element):
- lower_bound = max(1,3-1)=2
- upper_bound = min(3,1)=1
- 2 > 1 → no odd k → No

Is that correct? 
We have only one odd element and one even element.
We need to pick 3 elements. We can only pick at most 2 (1 odd + 1 even).
So we can't pick 3 elements → impossible? 

Wait, no: the array has only 3 elements. For x=3, we must pick all 3 elements.

Elements: one odd, one even, one other element.
If the third element is odd, then odd_count = 2, not 1.
In this case, if odd_count=1 and even_count=1, then total elements = 2, so x=3 is impossible.

Wait, this can't happen. The input constraint says n ≥ x.

So in the case where n=3, x=3, we have exactly 3 elements.

If we have only 1 odd element and 2 even elements:
- odd_count=1, even_count=2
- lower_bound = max(1,3-2)=1
- upper_bound = min(3,1)=1
- k=1 is odd → Yes

Selection: pick 1 odd, 2 even → sum is odd ✓

If we have 1 odd and 1 even, then total elements = 2, so x can't be 3.

So our constraints are valid because x ≤ n.

Therefore, the solution is correct.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even elements
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 0:
            even_count += 1
        else:
            odd_count += 1
    
    # If no odd elements, we cannot get an odd sum
    if odd_count == 0:
        print("No")
        continue
    
    # We need to find if there exists an odd k such that:
    # k is odd, k >= max(1, x - even_count), k <= min(x, odd_count)
    lower_bound = max(1, x - even_count)
    upper_bound = min(x, odd_count)
    
    # Check if there's any odd number in [lower_bound, upper_bound]
    found_odd = False
    for k in range(lower_bound, upper_bound + 1):
        if k % 2 == 1:
            found_odd = True
            break
    
    if found_odd:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even elements
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 0:
            even_count += 1
        else:
            odd_count += 1
    
    # If no odd elements, we cannot get an odd sum
    if odd_count == 0:
        print("No")
        continue
    
    # We need to find if there exists an odd k such that:
    # k is odd, k >= max(1, x - even_count), k <= min(x, odd_count)
    lower_bound = max(1, x - even_count)
    upper_bound = min(x, odd_count)
    
    # Check if there's any odd number in [lower_bound, upper_bound]
    found_odd = False
    for k in range(lower_bound, upper_bound + 1):
        if k % 2 == 1:
            found_odd = True
            break
    
    if found_odd:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if we can select exactly `x` elements from an array such that their sum is odd.

Key insights about odd/even sums:
- An odd number + even number = odd number
- An even number + even number = even number
- An odd number + odd number = even number

For a sum to be odd, we need an odd number of odd elements in our selection.

## Analysis

Let's think about when we can get an odd sum with exactly x elements:

1. **Count odd and even numbers** in the array
2. **We need an odd count of odd numbers** in our selection (1, 3, 5, ...)

So we need to check if we can pick exactly `x` elements with an odd number of them being odd.

## Cases to consider:

### Case 1: Can we even pick x elements?
Yes, since x ≤ n, we can always pick x elements.

### Case 2: Can we get an odd sum?
We need an odd number of odd elements in our selection.

Let's say we have:
- `odd_count` = number of odd elements in the array
- `even_count` = number of even elements in the array

We need to check if we can select exactly x elements such that the number of odd elements among them is odd.

The possible ways to have an odd number of odd elements:
- Pick 1 odd and (x-1) even elements
- Pick 3 odd and (x-3) even elements
- Pick 5 odd and (x-5) even elements
- etc.

For each odd count `k` (where k is odd and k ≤ odd_count), we need:
- k ≤ odd_count (we have enough odd elements)
- (x - k) ≤ even_count (we have enough even elements, since we're picking (x-k) even elements)

So we need to check if there exists any odd k such that:
- 0 ≤ k ≤ odd_count 
- 0 ≤ (x - k) ≤ even_count

Since k must be odd, k can be 1, 3, 5, ... up to the maximum odd number ≤ odd_count.

## Algorithm:

1. Count the number of odd elements in the array
2. For each odd number k from 1, 3, 5, ... up to min(odd_count, x):
   - Check if (x - k) ≤ even_count
   - If yes, we can form such a selection → return "Yes"
3. If no such k exists, return "No"

## Edge cases:
- If x = 0: sum is 0 (even), but x ≥ 1 per constraints
- If there are no odd elements: we can only get even sums, so answer is "No" unless x = 0 (not possible)
- If there are no even elements: we can only pick from odd elements

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** n=1, x=1, array=[999]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 0
- Try k=1: x-k = 1-1 = 0 ≤ 0 ✓ → Yes

**Example 2:** n=1, x=1, array=[1000]
- odd_count = 0, even_count = 1
- No odd k possible → No

**Example 3:** n=2, x=1, array=[51,50]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 1
- Try k=1: x-k = 1-1 = 0 ≤ 1 ✓ → Yes

**Example 4:** n=2, x=2, array=[51,50]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 1
- Try k=1: x-k = 2-1 = 1 ≤ 1 ✓ → Yes

**Example 5:** n=3, x=3, array=[101,102,103]
- odd_count = 2 (101,103), even_count = 1 (102)
- Try k=1: x-k = 3-1 = 2 > 1 ✗
- Try k=3: 3 > 2 (odd_count) ✗
- No valid k → No

This matches the expected output!

## Implementation:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even numbers
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 0:
            even_count += 1
        else:
            odd_count += 1
    
    # Check if we can form a selection of x elements with odd sum
    possible = False
    
    # Try all odd numbers of odd elements we can pick (1, 3, 5, ...)
    k = 1
    while k <= odd_count and k <= x:
        even_needed = x - k
        if even_needed <= even_count:
            possible = True
            break
        k += 2  # next odd number
    
    if possible:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to determine if we can select exactly `x` elements from an array such that their sum is odd.

Key insights about odd/even sums:
- Odd + Odd = Even
- Even + Even = Even  
- Odd + Even = Odd
- For a sum to be odd, we need an odd number of odd elements in our selection

## Analysis

For the sum of selected elements to be odd, we need an odd count of odd numbers among the selected `x` elements.

Let's think about the possible scenarios:

1. **If we have no odd numbers in the array**: All elements are even. Any sum of even numbers is even. So the answer is "No".

2. **If we have at least one odd number**: We can potentially get an odd sum by choosing an odd number of odd elements.

The key is whether we can achieve an odd count of odd elements when selecting exactly `x` elements.

## Strategy

Let's say:
- `odd_count` = number of odd elements in the array
- `even_count` = number of even elements in the array

We need to select exactly `x` elements with an odd number of odd elements.

For this to be possible:
1. If `odd_count == 0`: We can't get an odd sum (all elements are even) → "No"
2. If `odd_count > 0`: We can potentially create an odd sum, but we need to check if it's possible to select exactly `x` elements with an odd number of odd elements.

The possible ways to get an odd sum:
- Select 1 odd element and (x-1) even elements
- Select 3 odd elements and (x-3) even elements
- etc.

So we need at least 1 odd element available, and we need to be able to select an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...) such that the total count is exactly `x` and we don't exceed the available odd elements.

Conditions for success:
- If there are no odd elements → "No"
- Otherwise → we need to check if we can choose an odd number of odd elements where that odd number ≤ odd_count and (odd number) ≤ x

Actually, simpler approach:
- If we have at least one odd element, we can create an odd sum if we can select an odd number of odd elements (1, 3, 5, ...) and the remaining elements are even.

The minimum odd count we need is 1.

So we need:
- odd_count ≥ 1 (we have at least one odd element)
- We need to be able to select some odd count k where k is odd, k ≥ 1, k ≤ odd_count, and (x - k) ≤ even_count (we have enough even elements to fill the rest)

But wait - we can always choose exactly 1 odd element and (x-1) even elements, as long as:
- odd_count ≥ 1 (we have at least one odd element)
- even_count ≥ (x - 1) (we have enough even elements to make up the rest)

However, what if x = 1? Then we need exactly 1 odd element, which we have if odd_count ≥ 1.

What if x > 1? Then we need to be able to select at least 1 odd element and (x-1) even elements.

Actually, there's a simpler insight:
- If we have at least one odd element, we can make the sum odd if we can form a selection with exactly 1, 3, 5, ... odd elements.

The critical constraint is: can we have an odd number of odd elements in our selection?

We can have an odd number of odd elements if:
1. There is at least one odd element in the array, AND
2. We can choose some odd number k (1, 3, 5, ...) such that k ≤ odd_count and (x - k) ≤ even_count

But if x ≤ odd_count and x ≥ 1, then we can always choose 1 odd element and (x-1) even elements, provided we have enough even elements.

Actually, the most restrictive case is when we want to choose exactly 1 odd element. We need:
- odd_count ≥ 1 (we have at least one odd element)
- even_count ≥ (x - 1) (we have enough even elements to fill the rest after taking 1 odd element)

But what if we don't have enough even elements? Can we use more odd elements?

Yes! If we take 3 odd elements instead of 1, we need (x - 3) even elements. This works if (x - 3) ≤ even_count and x ≥ 3.

So we need to check if there exists an odd k such that:
- k ≥ 1, k ≤ odd_count, k ≤ x, and (x - k) ≤ even_count

But there's a simpler pattern: if odd_count ≥ 1, then we can achieve an odd sum if and only if we can find at least one odd k satisfying the above.

Actually, let me think of edge cases:
- If x = 1: we need exactly 1 odd element → possible if odd_count ≥ 1
- If x > 1: we need at least 1 odd element and enough even elements to make up the rest

But what if we don't have enough even elements? Can we use more odd elements?

Yes! For example, if we have 1 odd element and need to pick 5 elements, but only have 1 even element, we can't select 5 elements with an odd sum because:
- We need at least 1 odd element (we have it)
- If we pick 1 odd element, we need 4 even elements → we only have 1 → impossible
- If we pick 3 odd elements, we need 2 even elements → we only have 1 → impossible  
- If we pick 5 odd elements, impossible because we only have 1 odd element

So it's not always possible just because we have odd elements.

Let me reconsider: We need to check if there exists an odd number k (1, 3, 5, ...) such that:
- k ≤ odd_count
- k ≤ x
- (x - k) ≤ even_count

If such a k exists, answer is "Yes", otherwise "No".

But we can optimize this. We can try k = 1 first (the minimum odd number):
- k = 1: we need odd_count ≥ 1 and (x - 1) ≤ even_count

If this works, we're done. If not, we try k = 3, then k = 5, etc.

However, the maximum k we need to try is min(x, odd_count) and it must be odd.

But notice: as k increases, (x - k) decreases. So if k = 1 fails (meaning x - 1 > even_count), then for larger k, (x - k) will be even smaller, so it might work.

Wait: if x - 1 > even_count, then x - 3 > even_count only if x - 3 > x - 1, which is false. Actually, x - 3 < x - 1.

So if x - 1 > even_count, then x - 3 < x - 1, so x - 3 might be ≤ even_count.

For example: x = 5, even_count = 2
- k = 1: x - 1 = 4 > 2 → fails
- k = 3: x - 3 = 2 ≤ 2 → works!

So we need to check for the existence of any odd k where:
- k ≤ odd_count
- k ≤ x  
- x - k ≤ even_count

This is equivalent to: k ≥ x - even_count

So we need an odd k such that: max(1, x - even_count) ≤ k ≤ min(x, odd_count)

And k must be odd.

## Algorithm for each test case:

1. Count odd_count and even_count in the array
2. If odd_count == 0: return "No" (no odd elements, sum will always be even)
3. Find if there exists an odd integer k such that:
   - k ≥ max(1, x - even_count) 
   - k ≤ min(x, odd_count)
   - k is odd

If such k exists, print "Yes", else print "No".

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=1, x=1, array=[999]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 0
- k needs to satisfy: max(1, 1-0) = 1 ≤ k ≤ min(1,1) = 1, and k odd
- k=1 works → "Yes" ✓

Example 2: n=1, x=1, array=[1000]
- odd_count = 0 → "No" ✓

Example 3: n=2, x=1, array=[51,50]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 1
- k needs to satisfy: max(1, 1-1) = 1 ≤ k ≤ min(1,1) = 1, and k odd
- k=1 works → "Yes" ✓

Example 4: n=2, x=2, array=[51,50]
- odd_count = 1, even_count = 1
- k needs to satisfy: max(1, 2-1) = 1 ≤ k ≤ min(2,1) = 1, and k odd
- k=1 works → "Yes" ✓

Example 5: n=3, x=3, array=[101,102,103]
- odd_count = 2 (101,103), even_count = 1 (102)
- k needs to satisfy: max(1, 3-1) = 2 ≤ k ≤ min(3,2) = 2, and k odd
- k ≥ 2 and k ≤ 2 → k=2, but 2 is even → no odd k → "No" ✓

Perfect! This matches all examples.

## Implementation

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Count odd and even elements
    odd_count = 0
    even_count = 0
    for num in a:
        if num % 2 == 0:
            even_count += 1
        else:
            odd_count += 1
    
    # If no odd elements, sum will always be even
    if odd_count == 0:
        print("No")
        continue
    
    # Find if there exists an odd k such that:
    # k >= max(1, x - even_count)
    # k <= min(x, odd_count)
    # and k is odd
    
    min_k = max(1, x - even_count)
    max_k = min(x, odd_count)
    
    # Check if there's an odd number in [min_k, max_k]
    found = False
    for k in range(min_k, max_k + 1):
        if k % 2 == 1:  # k is odd
            found = True
            break
    
    if found:
        print("Yes")
    else:
        print("No")
```

Sereja has a sequence that consists of n positive integers, a1, a2, ..., an. 

First Sereja took a piece of squared paper and wrote all distinct non-empty non-decreasing subsequences of sequence a. Then for each sequence written on the squared paper, Sereja wrote on a piece of lines paper all sequences that do not exceed it.

A sequence of positive integers x = x1, x2, ..., xr doesn't exceed a sequence of positive integers y = y1, y2, ..., yr, if the following inequation holds: x1 ≤ y1, x2 ≤ y2, ..., xr ≤ yr.

Now Sereja wonders, how many sequences are written on the lines piece of paper. Help Sereja, find the required quantity modulo 1000000007 (109 + 7).

I think the key insight is that we can use a data structure to maintain the total sum of products of all currently active subsequences, and we update it as we go.

But if we have already seen sequences ending with value ≤ a[i], we need to know their total product sum.

Let's define:
- Let f(v) = total sum of products of all distinct non-decreasing subsequences ending with value exactly v
- Let g(v) = total sum of products of all distinct non-decreasing subsequences ending with value ≤ v

Then when processing a new element a[i]:
- We can extend all sequences ending with value ≤ a[i] by appending a[i], contributing a[i] × g(a[i])
- We also have the new sequence [a[i]] with product a[i]
- So total new contribution = a[i] + a[i] × g(a[i]) = a[i] × (1 + g(a[i]))

But let's trace through the example [1,2,2]:

Start: f={}, g={}, total=0

Process a[0]=1:
- new contribution = 1 × (1 + g(1)) = 1 × (1 + 0) = 1
- f(1) = 1
- g(1) = f(1) = 1
- total = 1

Process a[1]=2:
- new contribution = 2 × (1 + g(2)) = 2 × (1 + 0) = 2
- Now f(2) = 2 (from [2])
- g(2) = f(1) + f(2) = 1 + 2 = 3
- total = 1 + 2 = 3

Process a[2]=2:
- new contribution = 2 × (1 + g(2)) = 2 × (1 + 3) = 8
- f(2) becomes previous f(2) + 2 = 2 + 2 = 4
- g(2) becomes f(1) + f(2) = 1 + 4 = 5
- total = 3 + 8 = 11

But we only have one sequence [2] initially, then after first 2 we have f(2)=2 (only [2]), so extending gives [2,2] with product 4.

After second 2, we have two sequences ending with 2: the original [2] and the new [2], but we shouldn't have two [2]'s.

2. For each element, maintain the total sum of products of all non-decreasing subsequences ending at or before current value
